<div class="sidebar-list mw-collapsible mw-collapsed"><div class="sidebar-list-title">List</div><div class="sidebar-list-content mw-collapsible-content"><div class="hlist">

<li><a href="/wiki/Encyclical_of_the_Eastern_Patriarchs" title="Encyclical of the Eastern Patriarchs">Encyclical of the Eastern Patriarchs</a></li>

</div></div></div></td>
</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1063604349"><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Eastern_Orthodox_Church_sidebar" title="Template:Eastern Orthodox Church sidebar"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Eastern_Orthodox_Church_sidebar" title="Template talk:Eastern Orthodox Church sidebar"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Eastern_Orthodox_Church_sidebar&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Ephrem%27s_Commentary_on_the_Diatessaron.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/de/Ephrem%27s_Commentary_on_the_Diatessaron.jpg/220px-Ephrem%27s_Commentary_on_the_Diatessaron.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="183" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/de/Ephrem%27s_Commentary_on_the_Diatessaron.jpg/330px-Ephrem%27s_Commentary_on_the_Diatessaron.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/de/Ephrem%27s_Commentary_on_the_Diatessaron.jpg/440px-Ephrem%27s_Commentary_on_the_Diatessaron.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1600" data-file-height="1329" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Parchment" title="Parchment">Parchment</a> manuscript of the Ephrem's Commentary on the <a href="/wiki/Diatessaron" title="Diatessaron">Diatessaron</a>. Egypt, late 5th or early 6th century. <a href="/wiki/Chester_Beatty_Library" title="Chester Beatty Library">Chester Beatty Library</a></figcaption></figure>
<p><b>Ephrem the Syrian</b> (<a href="/wiki/Classical_Syriac_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Classical Syriac language">Classical Syriac</a>: <span lang="syc" dir="rtl">ܡܪܝ ܐܦܪܝܡ ܣܘܪܝܝܐ</span>, <small>romanized:&#160;</small><span title="Classical Syriac-language romanization"><i lang="syc-Latn">Mār ʾAp̄rêm Sūryāyā</i></span>, <small>Classical Syriac pronunciation:&#160;</small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="syc-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA" title="Help:IPA">[mɑr ʔafˈrem surˈjɑjɑ]</a></span>; <a href="/wiki/Koin%C4%93_Greek_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Koinē Greek language">Koinē Greek</a>: <span lang="grc">Ἐφραὶμ ὁ Σῦρος</span>, <small>romanized:&#160;</small><span title="Koinē Greek-language romanization"><i lang="grc-Latn">Efrém o Sýros</i></span>; <a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a>: <i lang="la">Ephraem Syrus</i>; <a href="/wiki/Amharic_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Amharic language">Amharic</a>: <span lang="am">ቅዱስ ኤፍሬም ሶርያዊ</span>; <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;306 – 373</span>), also known as <b>Saint Ephrem</b>, <b>Saint Ephraim</b>, <b>Ephrem of Edessa</b> or  <b>Aprem of Nisibis</b>, was a prominent <a href="/wiki/Christian_theology" title="Christian theology">Christian theologian</a> and <a href="/wiki/Christian_literature" title="Christian literature">writer</a>, who is revered as one of the most notable <a href="/wiki/Hymnographers" class="mw-redirect" title="Hymnographers">hymnographers</a> of <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Christianity" title="Eastern Christianity">Eastern Christianity</a>. He was born in <a href="/wiki/Nisibis" class="mw-redirect" title="Nisibis">Nisibis</a>, served as a <a href="/wiki/Deacon" title="Deacon">deacon</a> and later lived in <a href="/wiki/Edessa" title="Edessa">Edessa</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrock1992a_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrock1992a-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrock1999a_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrock1999a-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ephrem is venerated as a <a href="/wiki/Christian_saint" class="mw-redirect" title="Christian saint">saint</a> by all traditional Churches. He is especially revered in <a href="/wiki/Syriac_Christianity" title="Syriac Christianity">Syriac Christianity</a>, both in <a href="/wiki/East_Syriac_Rite" title="East Syriac Rite">East Syriac tradition</a> and <a href="/wiki/West_Syriac_Rite" title="West Syriac Rite">West Syriac tradition</a>, and also counted as a Holy and Venerable Father (i.e., a sainted Monk) in the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox Church</a>, especially in the Slovak Tradition. He was declared a Doctor of the Church in the Roman Catholic Church in 1920. Ephrem is also credited as the founder of the <a href="/wiki/School_of_Nisibis" title="School of Nisibis">School of Nisibis</a>, which, in later centuries, was the centre of learning of the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_the_East" title="Church of the East">Church of the East</a>.
</p><p>Ephrem wrote a wide variety of hymns, poems, and sermons in verse, as well as prose <a href="/wiki/Exegesis" title="Exegesis">exegesis</a>. These were works of practical theology for the edification of the <a href="/wiki/Christian_Church" title="Christian Church">Church</a> in troubled times. Some of these works have been examined by feminist scholars who have analyzed the incorporation of feminine imagery in his texts. They also examine the performance practice of all-women choirs singing his madrāšê, or his teaching hymns. Ephrem's works were so popular that, for centuries after his death, Christian authors wrote hundreds of <a href="/wiki/Pseudepigrapha" title="Pseudepigrapha">pseudepigraphal</a> works in his name. He has been called the most significant of all of the fathers of the <a href="/wiki/Syriac_language" title="Syriac language">Syriac</a>-speaking church tradition.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEParry1999180_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEParry1999180-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> In <a href="/wiki/Syriac_Christian" class="mw-redirect" title="Syriac Christian">Syriac Christian</a> tradition, he is considered patron of the <a href="/wiki/Arameans" title="Arameans">Syriac people</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Aramaic_roots_1-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Aramaic_roots-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Life">Life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ephrem_the_Syrian&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Dormition_of_Saint_Ephraim_the_Syrian_(Cretan,_15th_c.,_Byzantine_museum).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/92/Dormition_of_Saint_Ephraim_the_Syrian_%28Cretan%2C_15th_c.%2C_Byzantine_museum%29.jpg/220px-Dormition_of_Saint_Ephraim_the_Syrian_%28Cretan%2C_15th_c.%2C_Byzantine_museum%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/92/Dormition_of_Saint_Ephraim_the_Syrian_%28Cretan%2C_15th_c.%2C_Byzantine_museum%29.jpg/330px-Dormition_of_Saint_Ephraim_the_Syrian_%28Cretan%2C_15th_c.%2C_Byzantine_museum%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/92/Dormition_of_Saint_Ephraim_the_Syrian_%28Cretan%2C_15th_c.%2C_Byzantine_museum%29.jpg/440px-Dormition_of_Saint_Ephraim_the_Syrian_%28Cretan%2C_15th_c.%2C_Byzantine_museum%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1080" data-file-height="812" /></a><figcaption>Dormition of Saint Ephraim</figcaption></figure>
<p>Ephrem was born around the year 306 in the city of <a href="/wiki/Nisibis" class="mw-redirect" title="Nisibis">Nisibis</a> (modern <a href="/wiki/Nusaybin" title="Nusaybin">Nusaybin</a>, <a href="/wiki/Turkey" title="Turkey">Turkey</a>), in the Roman province of <a href="/wiki/Mesopotamia_(Roman_province)" title="Mesopotamia (Roman province)">Mesopotamia</a>, that was <a href="/wiki/Peace_of_Nisibis_(299)" title="Peace of Nisibis (299)">recently</a> acquired by the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Empire" title="Roman Empire">Roman Empire</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKarim20043_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarim20043-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPossekel19991_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPossekel19991-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELipiński200011_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELipiński200011-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERussell2005179-235_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERussell2005179-235-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> Internal evidence from Ephrem's hymnody suggests that both his parents were part of the growing Christian community in the city, although later <a href="/wiki/Hagiographer" class="mw-redirect" title="Hagiographer">hagiographers</a> wrote that his father was a pagan priest.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrock1992a16_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrock1992a16-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> In those days, religious culture in the region of Nisibis included local <a href="/wiki/Polytheism" title="Polytheism">polytheism</a>, <a href="/wiki/Judaism" title="Judaism">Judaism</a> and several varieties of the <a href="/wiki/Early_Christianity" title="Early Christianity">Early Christianity</a>. Most of the population spoke the <a href="/wiki/Aramaic_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Aramaic language">Aramaic language</a>, while <a href="/wiki/Classical_Greek" class="mw-redirect" title="Classical Greek">Greek</a> and <a href="/wiki/Latin" title="Latin">Latin</a> were languages of administration. The city had a complex ethnic composition, consisting of "Assyrians<i>, Arabs, Greeks, Jews, Parthians, Romans, and Iranians</i>".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcVey19895_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcVey19895-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Jacob_of_Nisibis" title="Jacob of Nisibis">Jacob</a>, the second <a href="/wiki/Bishop" title="Bishop">bishop</a> of Nisibis,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERussell2005220-222_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERussell2005220-222-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> was appointed in 308, and Ephrem grew up under his leadership of the community. Jacob of Nisibis is recorded as a signatory at the <a href="/wiki/First_Council_of_Nicea" class="mw-redirect" title="First Council of Nicea">First Council of Nicea</a> in 325. Ephrem was baptized as a youth and almost certainly became a <a href="/wiki/Members_of_the_Covenant" title="Members of the Covenant">son of the covenant</a>, an unusual form of Syriac proto-<a href="/wiki/Monasticism" title="Monasticism">monasticism</a>. Jacob appointed Ephrem as a teacher (Syriac <i>malp̄ānâ</i>, a title that still carries great respect for Syriac Christians). He was ordained as a <a href="/wiki/Deacon" title="Deacon">deacon</a> either at his baptism or later.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEParry1999180-181_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEParry1999180-181-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> He began to compose hymns and write biblical commentaries as part of his educational office. In his hymns, he sometimes refers to himself as a "herdsman" (<span title="Syriac-language text"><span lang="syr" dir="rtl">ܥܠܢܐ</span></span>, <i>‘allānâ</i>), to his bishop as the "shepherd" (<span title="Syriac-language text"><span lang="syr" dir="rtl">ܪܥܝܐ</span></span>, <i>rā‘yâ</i>), and to his community as a 'fold' (<span title="Syriac-language text"><span lang="syr" dir="rtl">ܕܝܪܐ</span></span>, <i>dayrâ</i>). Ephrem is popularly credited as the founder of the <a href="/wiki/School_of_Nisibis" title="School of Nisibis">School of Nisibis</a>, which, in later centuries, was the centre of learning of the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_the_East" title="Church of the East">Church of the East</a>.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Mar_Jacob_Church,_Nisibis.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/Mar_Jacob_Church%2C_Nisibis.jpg/220px-Mar_Jacob_Church%2C_Nisibis.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="126" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/Mar_Jacob_Church%2C_Nisibis.jpg/330px-Mar_Jacob_Church%2C_Nisibis.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/Mar_Jacob_Church%2C_Nisibis.jpg/440px-Mar_Jacob_Church%2C_Nisibis.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2144" data-file-height="1232" /></a><figcaption>Newly excavated <a href="/wiki/Church_of_Saint_Jacob_of_Nisibis" title="Church of Saint Jacob of Nisibis">Church of Saint Jacob of Nisibis</a>, where Ephrem taught and ministered</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 337, Emperor <a href="/wiki/Constantine_I_(emperor)" class="mw-redirect" title="Constantine I (emperor)">Constantine I</a>, who had legalised and promoted the practice of Christianity in the Roman Empire, died. Seizing on this opportunity, <a href="/wiki/Shapur_II" title="Shapur II">Shapur II</a> of Persia began a series of attacks into Roman North <a href="/wiki/Mesopotamia" title="Mesopotamia">Mesopotamia</a>. Nisibis was besieged in 338, 346 and 350. During the first siege, Ephrem credits Bishop Jacob as defending the city with his prayers. In the third siege, of 350, Shapur rerouted the River Mygdonius to undermine the walls of Nisibis. The Nisibenes quickly repaired the walls while the Persian elephant cavalry became bogged down in the wet ground. Ephrem celebrated what he saw as the miraculous salvation of the city in a hymn that portrayed Nisibis as being like <a href="/wiki/Noah%27s_Ark" title="Noah&#39;s Ark">Noah's Ark</a>, floating to safety on the flood.
</p><p>One important physical link to Ephrem's lifetime is the <a href="/wiki/Baptistery" title="Baptistery">baptistery</a> of Nisibis. The inscription tells that it was constructed under Bishop Vologeses in 359. In that year, Shapur attacked again. The cities around Nisibis were destroyed one by one, and their citizens killed or deported. <a href="/wiki/Constantius_II" title="Constantius II">Constantius II</a> was unable to respond; the campaign of <a href="/wiki/Julian_the_Apostate" class="mw-redirect" title="Julian the Apostate">Julian</a> in 363 ended with his death in battle. His army elected <a href="/wiki/Jovian_(Emperor)" class="mw-redirect" title="Jovian (Emperor)">Jovian</a> as the new emperor, and to rescue his army, he was forced to surrender Nisibis to Persia (also in 363) and to permit the expulsion of the entire Christian population.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERussell2005215,_217,_223_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERussell2005215,_217,_223-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> Ephrem declined being <a href="/wiki/Ordination" title="Ordination">ordinated</a> a bishop by <a href="/wiki/Feigning_madness" class="mw-redirect" title="Feigning madness">feigning madness</a>, because he regarded himself unworthy for it.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ephrem, with the others, went first to Amida (<a href="/wiki/Diyarbak%C4%B1r" title="Diyarbakır">Diyarbakır</a>), eventually settling in <a href="/wiki/Edessa,_Mesopotamia" class="mw-redirect" title="Edessa, Mesopotamia">Edessa</a> (Urhay, in Aramaic) in 363.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERussell2005195-196_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERussell2005195-196-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> Ephrem, in his late fifties, applied himself to ministry in his new church and seems to have continued his work as a teacher, perhaps in the <a href="/wiki/School_of_Edessa" title="School of Edessa">School of Edessa</a>. <a href="/wiki/Edessa" title="Edessa">Edessa</a> had been an important center of the Aramaic-speaking world, and the birthplace of a specific <a href="/wiki/Middle_Aramaic" class="mw-redirect" title="Middle Aramaic">Middle Aramaic</a> dialect that came to be known as the <a href="/wiki/Syriac_language" title="Syriac language">Syriac language</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHealey2007115–127_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHealey2007115–127-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> The city was rich with rivaling philosophies and religions. Ephrem comments that orthodox Nicene Christians were simply called "Palutians" in Edessa, after a former bishop. <a href="/wiki/Arianism" title="Arianism">Arians</a>, <a href="/wiki/Marcionism" title="Marcionism">Marcionites</a>, <a href="/wiki/Manichaeism" title="Manichaeism">Manichees</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bardaisan" title="Bardaisan">Bardaisanites</a> and various <a href="/wiki/Gnosticism" title="Gnosticism">gnostic sects</a> proclaimed themselves as the true church. In this confusion, Ephrem wrote a great number of hymns defending Nicene orthodoxy. A later Syriac writer, <a href="/wiki/Jacob_of_Serugh" title="Jacob of Serugh">Jacob of Serugh</a>, wrote that Ephrem rehearsed all-female choirs to sing his hymns set to Syriac folk tunes in the forum of Edessa. After a ten-year residency in Edessa, in his sixties, Ephrem succumbed to the plague as he ministered to its victims. The most reliable date for his death is 9 June 373.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Language">Language</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ephrem_the_Syrian&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Language">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Ephrem_miniature_16c.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/66/Ephrem_miniature_16c.jpg/220px-Ephrem_miniature_16c.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="347" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/66/Ephrem_miniature_16c.jpg/330px-Ephrem_miniature_16c.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/6/66/Ephrem_miniature_16c.jpg 2x" data-file-width="360" data-file-height="568" /></a><figcaption>Ephrem the Syriac in a 16th-century Russian illustration</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Nisibis_Church_interior.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/61/Nisibis_Church_interior.jpg/220px-Nisibis_Church_interior.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="126" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/61/Nisibis_Church_interior.jpg/330px-Nisibis_Church_interior.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/61/Nisibis_Church_interior.jpg/440px-Nisibis_Church_interior.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2144" data-file-height="1232" /></a><figcaption>The interior of the Church of <a href="/wiki/Jacob_of_Nisibis" title="Jacob of Nisibis">Saint Jacob</a> in <a href="/wiki/Nisibis" class="mw-redirect" title="Nisibis">Nisibis</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Ephrem wrote exclusively in his native <a href="/wiki/Aramaic_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Aramaic language">Aramaic language</a>, using the local Edessan (<i>Urhaya</i>) dialect, that later came to be known as the <a href="/wiki/Classical_Syriac" class="mw-redirect" title="Classical Syriac">Classical Syriac</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrock1992a16_9-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrock1992a16-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrock1999a105_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrock1999a105-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> Ephrem's works contain several <a href="/wiki/Endonymic" class="mw-redirect" title="Endonymic">endonymic</a> (native) references to his language (<i>Aramaic</i>), homeland (<i>Aram</i>) and people (<i>Arameans</i>).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGriffith200215,_20_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGriffith200215,_20-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPalmer20033_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPalmer20033-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGriffith2006447_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGriffith2006447-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDebié2009103_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDebié2009103-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMesso2011119_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMesso2011119-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> He is therefore known as "<i>the authentic voice of Aramaic Christianity</i>".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESimmons195913_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESimmons195913-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the early stages of modern scholarly studies, it was believed that some examples of the long-standing Greek practice of labeling Aramaic as "Syriac", that are found in the <i>"<a href="/wiki/Cave_of_Treasures" title="Cave of Treasures">Cave of Treasures</a>",</i><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEToepel2013540-584_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEToepel2013540-584-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWood2007131-140_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWood2007131-140-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> can be attributed to Ephrem, but later scholarly analyses have shown that the work in question was written much later (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 600) by an unknown author, thus also showing that Ephrem's original works still belonged to the tradition unaffected by <a href="/wiki/Exonymic" class="mw-redirect" title="Exonymic">exonymic</a> (foreign) labeling.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERubin1998322-323_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERubin1998322-323-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEToepel2013531-539_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEToepel2013531-539-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMinov2013157-165_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMinov2013157-165-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERuzer2014196-197_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERuzer2014196-197-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>One of the early admirers of Ephrem's works, theologian <a href="/wiki/Jacob_of_Serugh" title="Jacob of Serugh">Jacob of Serugh</a> (d. 521), who already belonged to the generation that accepted the custom of a double naming of their language not only as Aramaic (<i>Ārāmāyā</i>) but also as "Syriac" (<i>Suryāyā</i>),<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrock1992c226_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrock1992c226-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERompay200078_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERompay200078-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEButts2011390-391_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEButts2011390-391-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEButts2019222_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEButts2019222-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> wrote a <a href="/wiki/Homily" title="Homily">homily</a> (<i>memrā</i>) dedicated to Ephrem, praising him as the <i>crown</i> or <i>wreath</i> of the <i>Arameans</i> (<a href="/wiki/Classical_Syriac_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Classical Syriac language">Classical Syriac</a>: <span lang="syc" dir="rtl">ܐܳܪܳܡܳܝܘܬܐ</span>), and the same praise was repeated in early liturgical texts.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmar199564-65_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmar199564-65-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrock1999b14-15_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrock1999b14-15-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> Only later, under the Greek influence already prevalent in the works of the middle fifth century author <a href="/wiki/Theodoret_of_Cyrus" class="mw-redirect" title="Theodoret of Cyrus">Theodoret of Cyrus</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAzéma1965190-191_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAzéma1965190-191-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> did it became customary to associate Ephrem with Syriac identity, and label him only as "the Syrian" (<a href="/wiki/Koin%C4%93_Greek_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Koinē Greek language">Koinē Greek</a>: <span lang="grc">Ἐφραίμ ὁ Σῦρος</span>), thus blurring his Aramaic <a href="/wiki/Self-identification" class="mw-redirect" title="Self-identification">self-identification</a>, attested by his own writings and works of other Aramaic-speaking writers, and also by examples from the earliest liturgical tradition.
</p><p>Some of those problems persisted up to the recent times, even in scholarly literature, as a consequence of several methodological problems within the field of <a href="/wiki/Historical_source" title="Historical source">source</a> editing. During the process of critical editing and translation of sources within <a href="/wiki/Syriac_studies" title="Syriac studies">Syriac studies</a>, some scholars have practiced various forms of arbitrary (and often unexplained) interventions, including the occasional disregard for the importance of original terms, used as <a href="/wiki/Endonymic" class="mw-redirect" title="Endonymic">endonymic</a> (native) designations for Arameans and their language (ārāmāyā). Such disregard was manifested primarily in translations and commentaries, by replacement of authentic terms with <a href="/wiki/Polysemic" class="mw-redirect" title="Polysemic">polysemic</a> Syrian/Syriac labels. In previously mentioned <i>memrā</i>, dedicated to Ephrem, one of the terms for <i>Aramean people</i> (<a href="/wiki/Classical_Syriac_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Classical Syriac language">Classical Syriac</a>: <span lang="syc" dir="rtl">ܐܳܪܳܡܳܝܘܬܐ</span> / Arameandom) was published correctly in original script of the source,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmar199564_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmar199564-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> but in the same time it was translated in English as <i>"Syriac nation",</i><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmar199565_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmar199565-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> and then enlisted among quotations related to "Syrian/Syriac" identity,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmar199521_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmar199521-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> without any mention of Aramean-related terms in the source. Even when noticed and corrected by some scholars,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrock1999b15_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrock1999b15-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERompay200499_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERompay200499-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMinov2020304_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMinov2020304-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> such replacements of terms continue to create problems for others.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWood2012186_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWood2012186-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMinov2013160_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMinov2013160-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Several translations of his writings exist in <a href="/wiki/Classical_Armenian_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Classical Armenian language">Classical Armenian</a>, <a href="/wiki/Coptic_language" title="Coptic language">Coptic</a>, <a href="/wiki/Old_Georgian_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Old Georgian language">Old Georgian</a>, <a href="/wiki/Koine_Greek" title="Koine Greek">Koine Greek</a> and other languages. Some of his works are extant only in translation (particularly in Armenian).
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Writings">Writings</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ephrem_the_Syrian&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Writings">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Over four hundred <a href="/wiki/Hymn" title="Hymn">hymns</a> composed by Ephrem still exist. Granted that some have been lost, Ephrem's productivity is not in doubt. The church historian <a href="/wiki/Sozomen" title="Sozomen">Sozomen</a> credits Ephrem with having written over three million lines. Ephrem combines in his writing a threefold heritage: he draws on the models and methods of early <a href="/wiki/Rabbinic_Judaism" title="Rabbinic Judaism">Rabbinic Judaism</a>, he engages skillfully with Greek science and philosophy, and he delights in the Mesopotamian/Persian tradition of mystery symbolism.
</p><p>The most important of his works are his lyric, teaching hymns (ܡܕܖ̈ܫܐ, <i>madrāšê</i>). These hymns are full of rich, poetic imagery drawn from biblical sources, folk tradition, and other religions and philosophies. The madrāšê are written in stanzas of <a href="/wiki/Syllabic_verse" title="Syllabic verse">syllabic verse</a> and employ over fifty different metrical schemes. The form is defined by an <a href="/wiki/Antiphon" title="Antiphon">antiphon</a>, or congregational <a href="/wiki/Refrain" title="Refrain">refrain</a> (ܥܘܢܝܬܐ, <i>‘ûnîṯâ</i>), between each independent strophe (or verse), and the refrain's melody mimics that of the opening half of the strophe.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> Each madrāšâ had its <i>qālâ</i> (ܩܠܐ), a traditional tune identified by its opening line. All of these qālê are now lost. It seems that <a href="/wiki/Bardaisan" title="Bardaisan">Bardaisan</a> and <a href="/wiki/Mani_(prophet)" title="Mani (prophet)">Mani</a> composed madrāšê, and Ephrem felt that the medium was a suitable tool to use against their claims. The madrāšê are gathered into various hymn cycles. Each group has a title — <i>Carmina Nisibena</i>, <i>On Faith</i>, <i>On Paradise</i>, <i>On Virginity</i>, <i>Against Heresies</i> — but some of these titles do not do justice to the entirety of the collection (for instance, only the first half of the <i>Carmina Nisibena</i> is about Nisibis). Some of these hymn cycles provide implicit insight into Ephrem's perceived level of comfort with incorporating feminine imagery into his writings. One such hymn cycle was <i>Hymns on the Nativity</i>, centered around Mary, which contained 28 hymns and had the clearest pervasive theme of Ephrem's hymn cycles.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_48-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> An example of feminine imagery is found when Ephrem writes of the baby Jesus: "he was lofty but he sucked Mary's milk and from his blessings all creation sucks."<sup id="cite_ref-:0_48-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Particularly influential were his <i>Hymns Against Heresies</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGriffith199997-114_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGriffith199997-114-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> Ephrem used these to warn his flock of the heresies that threatened to divide the early church. He lamented that the faithful were "tossed to and fro and carried around with every wind of doctrine, by the cunning of men, by their craftiness and deceitful wiles" (Eph 4:14).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMourachian200730-31_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMourachian200730-31-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> He devised hymns laden with doctrinal details to inoculate right-thinking Christians against heresies such as <a href="/wiki/Docetism" title="Docetism">docetism</a>. The <i>Hymns Against Heresies</i> employ colourful metaphors to describe the Incarnation of Christ as fully human and divine. Ephrem asserts that Christ's unity of humanity and divinity represents peace, perfection and salvation; in contrast, docetism and other heresies sought to divide or reduce Christ's nature and, in doing so, rend and devalue Christ's followers with their false teachings.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Performance_Practices_and_Gender">Performance Practices and Gender</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ephrem_the_Syrian&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Performance Practices and Gender">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The relationship between Ephrem's compositions and femininity is shown again in documentation suggesting that the madrāšê were sung by all-women choirs with an accompanying lyre. These women's choirs were composed of members of the Daughters of the Covenant, an important institution in historical <a href="/wiki/Syriac_Christianity" title="Syriac Christianity">Syriac Christianity</a>, but they weren't always labeled as such.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> Ephrem, like many Syriac liturgical poets, believed that women's voices were important to hear in the church as they were modeled after Mary, mother of Jesus, whose acceptance of God's call led to salvation for all through the birth of Jesus.<sup id="cite_ref-:2_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> One variety of the madrāšê, the <i>soghyatha</i>, was sung in a conversational style between male and female choirs.<sup id="cite_ref-:2_52-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> The women's choir would sing the role of biblical women, and the men's choir would sing the male role. Through the role of singing Ephrem's madrāšê, women's choirs were granted a role in worship.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_51-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Further_Writings">Further Writings</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ephrem_the_Syrian&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Further Writings">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Ephrem also wrote verse homilies (<span title="Syriac-language text"><span lang="syr" dir="rtl">ܡܐܡܖ̈ܐ</span></span>, <i>mêmrê</i>). These sermons in poetry are far fewer in number than the madrāšê. The mêmrê were written in a heptosyllabic <a href="/wiki/Couplet" title="Couplet">couplets</a> (pairs of lines of seven syllables each).
</p><p>The third category of Ephrem's writings is his prose work. He wrote a biblical commentary on the <a href="/wiki/Diatessaron" title="Diatessaron">Diatessaron</a> (the single gospel harmony of the early Syriac church), the Syriac original of which was found in 1957. His <i>Commentary on Genesis and Exodus</i> is an exegesis of <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Genesis" title="Book of Genesis">Genesis</a> and <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Exodus" title="Book of Exodus">Exodus</a>. Some fragments exist in Armenian of his commentaries on the <a href="/wiki/Acts_of_the_Apostles" title="Acts of the Apostles">Acts of the Apostles</a> and <a href="/wiki/Pauline_Epistles" class="mw-redirect" title="Pauline Epistles">Pauline Epistles</a>.
</p><p>He also wrote refutations against <a href="/wiki/Bardaisan" title="Bardaisan">Bardaisan</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mani_(prophet)" title="Mani (prophet)">Mani</a>, <a href="/wiki/Marcion_of_Sinope" title="Marcion of Sinope">Marcion</a> and others.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMitchell1912_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMitchell1912-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMitchellBevanBurkitt1921_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMitchellBevanBurkitt1921-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Syriac churches still use many of Ephrem's hymns as part of the annual cycle of worship. However, most of these liturgical hymns are edited and conflated versions of the originals.
</p><p>The most complete, critical text of authentic Ephrem was compiled between 1955 and 1979 by Dom Edmund Beck, OSB, as part of the <i>Corpus Scriptorum Christianorum Orientalium</i>.
</p><p>Ephrem is attributed with writing <a href="/wiki/Hagiography" title="Hagiography">hagiographies</a> such as <i><a href="/wiki/The_Life_of_Saint_Mary_the_Harlot" title="The Life of Saint Mary the Harlot">The Life of Saint Mary the Harlot</a></i>, though this credit is called into question.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrockHarvey1998_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrockHarvey1998-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>One of the works attributed to Ephrem was the <i><a href="/wiki/Cave_of_Treasures" title="Cave of Treasures">Cave of Treasures</a></i>, written by a much later but unknown author, who lived at the end of the 6th and the beginning of the 7th century.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEToepel2013531-584_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEToepel2013531-584-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Symbols_and_metaphors">Symbols and metaphors</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ephrem_the_Syrian&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Symbols and metaphors">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Ephrem's writings contain a rich variety of symbols and metaphors. Christopher Buck gives a summary of analysis of a selection of six key scenarios (the way, robe of glory, <a href="/wiki/Sons_and_daughters_of_the_Covenant" class="mw-redirect" title="Sons and daughters of the Covenant">sons and daughters of the Covenant</a>, wedding feast, <a href="/wiki/Harrowing_of_hell" class="mw-redirect" title="Harrowing of hell">harrowing of hell</a>, <a href="/wiki/Noah%E2%80%99s_Ark" class="mw-redirect" title="Noah’s Ark">Noah’s Ark</a>/Mariner) and six root metaphors (physician, medicine of life, mirror, pearl, <a href="/wiki/Tree_of_life" title="Tree of life">Tree of life</a>, <a href="/wiki/Paradise" title="Paradise">paradise</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBuck199977–109_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBuck199977–109-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Greek_Ephrem">Greek Ephrem</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ephrem_the_Syrian&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Greek Ephrem">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Ephrem's meditations on the symbols of Christian faith and his stand against heresy made him a popular source of inspiration throughout the church. There is a huge corpus of Ephrem pseudepigraphy and legendary <a href="/wiki/Hagiography" title="Hagiography">hagiography</a> in many languages. Some of these compositions are in verse, often mimicking Ephrem's heptasyllabic couplets.
</p><p>There is a very large number of works by "Ephrem" extant in Greek. In the literature this material is often referred to as "Greek Ephrem", or <i>Ephraem Graecus</i> (as opposed to the real Ephrem the Syrian), as if it was by a single author. This is not the case, but the term is used for convenience. Some texts are in fact Greek translations of genuine works by Ephrem. Most are not. The best known of these writings is the <i><a href="/wiki/Prayer_of_Saint_Ephrem" title="Prayer of Saint Ephrem">Prayer of Saint Ephrem</a></i>, which is recited at every service during <a href="/wiki/Great_Lent" title="Great Lent">Great Lent</a> and other fasting periods in <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Christianity" title="Eastern Christianity">Eastern Christianity</a>.
</p><p>There are also works by "Ephrem" in <a href="/wiki/Latin" title="Latin">Latin</a>, <a href="/wiki/Slavonic_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Slavonic language">Slavonic</a> and <a href="/wiki/Arabic_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Arabic language">Arabic</a>. "Ephrem Latinus" is the term given to Latin translations of "Ephrem Graecus". None is by Ephrem the Syrian. "Pseudo Ephrem Latinus" is the name given to Latin works under the name of Ephrem which are imitations of the style of Ephrem Latinus.
</p><p>There has been very little critical examination of any of these works. They were edited uncritically by Assemani, and there is also a modern Greek edition by Phrantzolas.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Veneration_as_a_saint">Veneration as a saint</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ephrem_the_Syrian&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Veneration as a saint">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:George_John_Ephraim_Triptychon_fragment_Sinai_14th_century.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e1/George_John_Ephraim_Triptychon_fragment_Sinai_14th_century.jpg/220px-George_John_Ephraim_Triptychon_fragment_Sinai_14th_century.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="472" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e1/George_John_Ephraim_Triptychon_fragment_Sinai_14th_century.jpg/330px-George_John_Ephraim_Triptychon_fragment_Sinai_14th_century.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e1/George_John_Ephraim_Triptychon_fragment_Sinai_14th_century.jpg/440px-George_John_Ephraim_Triptychon_fragment_Sinai_14th_century.jpg 2x" data-file-width="726" data-file-height="1558" /></a><figcaption>Saints Ephrem (right) <a href="/wiki/St._George" class="mw-redirect" title="St. George">George</a> (top) and <a href="/wiki/St._John_Damascene" class="mw-redirect" title="St. John Damascene">John Damascene</a> on a 14th-century triptych</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Ic%C3%B4ne_Ephrem_le_Syrien.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/Ic%C3%B4ne_Ephrem_le_Syrien.jpg/220px-Ic%C3%B4ne_Ephrem_le_Syrien.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="294" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/Ic%C3%B4ne_Ephrem_le_Syrien.jpg/330px-Ic%C3%B4ne_Ephrem_le_Syrien.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/Ic%C3%B4ne_Ephrem_le_Syrien.jpg/440px-Ic%C3%B4ne_Ephrem_le_Syrien.jpg 2x" data-file-width="762" data-file-height="1019" /></a><figcaption>Contemporary Romanian icon (2005)</figcaption></figure>
<p>Soon after Ephrem's death, legendary accounts of his life began to circulate. One of the earlier "modifications" is the statement that Ephrem's father was a pagan priest of Abnil or Abizal. However, internal evidence from his authentic writings suggest that he was raised by Christian parents.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ephrem is venerated as an example of monastic discipline in <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Christianity" title="Eastern Christianity">Eastern Christianity</a>. In the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox</a> scheme of hagiography, Ephrem is counted as a Venerable Father (i.e., a sainted monk). His <a href="/wiki/Feast_day" class="mw-redirect" title="Feast day">feast day</a> is celebrated on 28 January and on the Saturday of the Venerable Fathers (<a href="/wiki/Cheesefare" class="mw-redirect" title="Cheesefare">Cheesefare</a> Saturday), which is the Saturday before the beginning of <a href="/wiki/Great_Lent" title="Great Lent">Great Lent</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 5 October 1920, Pope <a href="/wiki/Benedict_XV" class="mw-redirect" title="Benedict XV">Benedict XV</a> proclaimed Ephrem a <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_the_Church" title="Doctor of the Church">Doctor of the Church</a> ("Doctor of the Syrians").<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The most popular title for Ephrem is <i>Harp of the Spirit</i> (Syriac: <span title="Syriac-language text"><span lang="syr" dir="rtl">ܟܢܪܐ ܕܪܘܚܐ</span></span>, <i>Kenārâ d-Rûḥâ</i>). He is also referred to as the Deacon of Edessa, the Sun of the Syrians and a Pillar of the Church.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His Roman Catholic feast day of 9 June conforms to his date of death. For 48 years (1920–1969), it was on 18 June, and this date is still observed in the Extraordinary Form.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ephrem is honored with a <a href="/wiki/Feast_day" class="mw-redirect" title="Feast day">feast day</a> on the <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Episcopal_Church_in_the_United_States_of_America)" class="mw-redirect" title="Calendar of saints (Episcopal Church in the United States of America)">liturgical calendar of the Episcopal Church (USA)</a> on June 10.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ephrem is <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Church_of_England)" title="Calendar of saints (Church of England)">remembered</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a> with a <a href="/wiki/Commemoration_(observance)" class="mw-redirect" title="Commemoration (observance)">commemoration</a> on <a href="/wiki/June_9" title="June 9">9 June</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
